For example, more discussion does not necessarily mean better communication, and a rise in reported problems may reflect improved willingness to speak up rather than worsening performance. Teams should combine quantitative measures with debriefs and direct feedback, then adjust their practices based on observed bottlenecks.
Study instruction:
- Build a small team-effectiveness scorecard with process and outcome measures, including at least one measure of information sharing and one of task quality.
Key point:
- Effective group cognition should be evaluated through behavior and results.
- Metrics need context and should not reward superficial activity.
70. Q: How can a team protect its transactive memory when an expert leaves or changes roles?
A: Show answer
A team should avoid allowing essential knowledge to exist only in one person's memory. Before a specialist leaves, the organization should identify the domains they maintain, the decisions they influence, the recurring questions they answer, and the records they use. Critical procedures and explanations should be documented in shared repositories, and a successor or backup should be given opportunities to perform the work with support. The expertise directory must then be updated so that colleagues know where to direct future questions. This process should preserve judgment and context, not merely copy files. Some tacit expertise requires mentoring, demonstrations, and supervised practice. Succession planning is therefore both a knowledge-management activity and a group-cognition safeguard.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a knowledge-transfer plan for a critical role that includes documents, demonstrations, shadowing, backup ownership, and directory updates.
Key point:
- A knowledge map becomes unreliable when role changes are not reflected.
- Transfer requires both records and practical learning.
71. Q: What can the Palau case teach teams about communication during a high-stakes emergency?
A: Show answer
The Palau emergency shows that teams must preserve a shared picture of the situation while distributing urgent actions. Critical information should be communicated clearly, acknowledged, and passed to the people who need it. Roles must be understood, but the system must also be flexible enough to reorganize when equipment or normal procedures fail. Team members need to report uncertainty and abnormal conditions promptly rather than wait until they have a complete explanation. Leaders should maintain composure without masking the seriousness of the situation. After the event, a debrief should identify which communication paths worked, where information was delayed, and which backup procedures need improvement. The case is an illustration of system-level coordination, not a substitute for domain-specific emergency training.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a tabletop exercise in which one critical system fails and require the team to maintain shared situational awareness while reallocating tasks.
Key point:
- Emergency coordination depends on clear information flow and adaptive roles.
- Rehearsed backup procedures reduce dependence on improvisation.
72. Q: How can shared attention and transactive memory work together in a complex task?
A: Show answer
Shared attention helps the group establish what issue or information currently matters, while transactive memory helps members identify who has the knowledge needed to interpret or act on it. For example, a team may jointly review an unexpected project variance, then direct questions to the finance specialist, the program manager, or the procurement expert according to the nature of the issue. The shared focus prevents members from discussing unrelated versions of the problem; the expertise map ensures that the right knowledge enters the discussion. Both mechanisms are necessary: a common focus without access to specialist knowledge can produce shallow analysis, while a directory of experts without a shared understanding of the issue can lead to fragmented responses.
Study instruction:
- Trace a decision from the moment the issue is jointly identified through the routing of questions to relevant experts and the integration of their advice.
Key point:
- Shared attention establishes the common problem.
- Transactive memory connects that problem to the right expertise.
73. Q: Why must a shared artifact remain revisable rather than becoming a fixed record?
A: Show answer
Complex tasks evolve as new evidence emerges, assumptions are tested, and decisions change. A fixed representation can become misleading if it continues to display an outdated plan as though it were current. A revisable artifact allows the group to incorporate new information while preserving enough history to understand why changes were made. Version history, decision logs, dates, and clear ownership help distinguish current status from earlier proposals. Revisability should not mean that anyone can silently overwrite a critical record. Changes to important decisions should be attributable and, where necessary, approved. The goal is a living representation that supports adaptation without losing traceability.
Study instruction:
- Review a project tracker or diagram and specify how updates, approvals, version history, and archived decisions should be handled.
Key point:
- Shared artifacts must evolve with the task while retaining traceability.
- Controlled revision prevents stale information and silent changes.
74. Q: What risks arise when a team depends too heavily on one knowledge champion?
A: Show answer
A knowledge champion can provide valuable specialist depth, but excessive dependence creates a single point of failure. The person may become unavailable, leave the organization, become overloaded, or make an error that others cannot recognize. Other members may stop developing their own understanding and may defer even when the specialist's advice is outside the relevant scope. Teams should maintain backup expertise, document key procedures, invite peer review, and periodically test whether knowledge can be accessed without the champion. The meta-knowledge function should make the expertise network visible rather than concentrate all access through one individual. Specialization is useful when it is connected to resilience and shared accountability.
Study instruction:
- Identify critical knowledge domains with only one capable owner and create a backup, documentation, and cross-training plan.
Key point:
- Specialization should not become an organizational dependency risk.
- Redundancy and knowledge transfer strengthen continuity.
75. Q: How can leaders create shared fate without suppressing individual contribution?
A: Show answer
Leaders can define a meaningful collective outcome while keeping individual responsibilities transparent. Team members should understand how their own work contributes to the shared result and how dependencies affect colleagues. Recognition can include both individual expertise and the team's integrated achievement. Performance measures should discourage withholding information or optimizing local targets at the expense of the overall outcome. At the same time, shared responsibility should not allow poor performance to disappear into the group. Clear ownership, fair workload allocation, and honest feedback are necessary. The balance is achieved when members are accountable for their roles and also understand that the group's success depends on helping one another succeed.
Study instruction:
- Redesign one team objective so that it includes a collective outcome, clear individual accountabilities, and recognition for cross-role assistance.
Key point:
- Shared fate aligns effort around a common result.
- Individual ownership remains necessary for accountability.
76. Q: How can the chapter's ideas be used to improve a recurring meeting?
A: Show answer
Start by defining the decision or shared work the meeting must accomplish. Ask participants to prepare independent observations before the meeting, then collect written contributions so that the discussion does not begin with the most senior person's view. Display one authoritative shared artifact, identify the relevant expertise in the room, and use structured turn-taking to surface unique information. Acknowledge and restate important contributions, clarify disagreements, and record decisions, owners, and deadlines. Use a short check-in or shared ritual when relationship-building is relevant, but avoid adding activities that do not serve the purpose. Afterward, evaluate whether the meeting improved understanding and produced action rather than measuring success by its length or number of attendees.
Study instruction:
- Redesign a real meeting agenda using silent preparation, shared artifacts, inclusive turn-taking, expertise routing, and documented follow-up.
Key point:
- Meeting quality depends on how information is surfaced and integrated.
- Clear outputs and follow-through matter more than attendance alone.
77. Q: What is the central lesson of Chapter 9, “Thinking with Groups”?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that human thinking can extend beyond the individual through coordinated relationships with other people. Groups can combine specialized knowledge, direct shared attention, motivate members through belonging, and make reasoning visible through conversation and shared artifacts. Synchrony, shared arousal, learning, training, emotional exchange, and rituals can help create the cohesion needed for this work. But cohesion alone is insufficient: groups must also distribute participation, surface unique information, maintain transactive memory, align incentives, and protect dissent. The Palau crew and the jigsaw classroom illustrate different forms of this principle: one in an emergency and one in education. The broader conclusion is that collective intelligence is not automatic. It emerges when people deliberately organize their interactions so that each person's knowledge can contribute to a shared result.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter through one example of distributed cognition, one mechanism of cohesion, and one practical design rule for improving group work.
Key point:
- Thinking with groups is a learnable and designable human capability.
- Collective intelligence requires both connection and disciplined information integration.
Rows: 77
